# SpatioTemporal Tiles as a family of optimization problems

_2026-08-10 · derived from a whole-repo read of the Rust build/format/optimize crates (`stt-core`, `stt-build`, `stt-optimize`) and the data-delivery TypeScript packages (`@poopdeck.gl/core`, `@poopdeck.gl/playback`), plus `docs/spec` and the decision records in `docs/roadmap`._

Every load-bearing decision this project makes — where a feature's bytes live in the zoom pyramid, how wide a temporal bucket is, how many bits a coordinate gets, what order blobs are laid down in, which tile to evict, when the playhead may advance — is an optimization problem that the code currently solves with a heuristic, a fixed rule, or a constant. This document states those problems **formally**: 66 problems across 13 subsystems, each with the objective, decision variables, and constraints written down, the shipped code framed as the incumbent solution, and the measurable gap a better solver could close.

The point is not that every incumbent should be replaced — many are the right engineering answer — but that writing the true problem down exposes exactly _where_ each heuristic is greedy, separable, or hardcoded, which constraints are load-bearing (several of the best design decisions here are **rejections**, recorded in §13 and §6.1), and which knobs are actually coupled when the advisors treat them as independent.

## Scope

Core competencies only: encoding, tiling, bucketing, packing, ordering, indexing, LOD/aggregation, caching, prefetch, scheduling, playback QoS, and parameter auto-tuning. **Rendering is out of scope** — STT sits on deck.gl / three / maplibre / cesium, so shaders, draw calls, GPU state, and frame pacing belong to those layers (and `packages/core/src/render/*` is renderer-side despite its location). Where a concept straddles the boundary (tile selection feeds the renderer; scrub-LOD trades visual staleness), only the data-delivery side is formalized.

## How each entry reads

Every problem follows the same skeleton:

- **Setting** — what the system actually does, with `file:line` citations.
- **Problem** — the complete formal statement: given / decision variables / objective / constraints. Symbols are defined locally; each subsection is self-contained.
- **Incumbent** — the shipped algorithm, framed as a feasible (usually greedy or fixed-rule) point of the formal problem.
- **Class** — the known problem family it maps to (knapsack, min linear arrangement, online scheduling, Belady/MIN, rate–distortion, coordinate descent, …) and complexity notes.
- **Knobs** — the tunable parameters and their defaults.
- **Gap** — what a principled solver could win, and the measurable objective it moves. Ideas that were considered and set aside are marked **PLANNED**, **DEFERRED**, or **REJECTED** — rejected designs are kept deliberately, with reasons, as constraint documentation.

Shared conventions: $F$ is the feature set, $z$ a zoom level, $T_z$ the Web-Mercator tile grid at zoom $z$, $t$ a tile, $\tau$ or $\Delta t$ a temporal bucket, $B(\cdot)$ or $\ell$ byte costs, and $w_q$ workload weights over queries $q$. Everything else is defined where used.

## 1. Spatial tiling & the zoom pyramid

The build pipeline (`stt-build`) decides where every feature's geometry bytes live in a Web-Mercator zoom pyramid: which tiles at which zooms store which clipped, simplified copy of each feature, and how the encode work that produces them is scheduled. The resources traded are archive bytes (replication across zooms and across tile seams) against query completeness, rendered fidelity at each zoom's pixel scale, and build wall-clock/memory. Sibling decisions made in the same code path are owned elsewhere: temporal bucketing, adaptive windowing, and the temporal-LOD pyramid (see §2 — the temporal axis), the per-tile feature-drop budget (see §4 — importance knapsack under count + byte caps), on-disk blob ordering via space-filling curves (see §6 — minimum linear arrangement under range-coalescing cost), and the `min_zoom`/`max_zoom` clamp itself, whose selection algorithm lives in `stt-optimize` (see §12 — zoom-range recommendation).

### 1.1 Multi-zoom feature-to-tile assignment (replication vs. query completeness)

**Setting.** The build re-places every feature independently at every zoom in `[min_zoom, max_zoom]` (loop at tiler.rs:474-494; per-zoom parallel placement in `process_zoom_level`, tiler.rs:1364-1442). The single placement authority `place_feature` (tiler.rs:1329-1362) routes timed LineStrings through the trajectory clipper — one clipped segment per touched tile, enumerated by an Amanatides–Woo supercover rather than a bbox sweep (clip.rs:207-336) — (Multi)Polygons through a per-tile Sutherland–Hodgman coverage clip over the bbox tile range (tiler.rs:815-1010, clip.rs:1075-1180), and points into exactly one tile (projection.rs:57-85). A feature is thus stored once per (zoom, touched tile): full-pyramid replication with no cross-zoom sharing. Two gates prune the extremes: `min_features_per_tile` drops sub-threshold deep tiles, betting on the client's parent fallback (tiler.rs:619-623, config doc tiler.rs:121-124), and per-feature zoom bands `min_zoom_field`/`max_zoom_field` skip a feature outside its data-declared band before any clip (tiler.rs:714-726, 1377-1382).

**Problem.** Given: feature set $F$; zoom range $Z = \{z_{min},\dots,z_{max}\}$; the Web-Mercator tile grid $T_z$ at zoom $z$ ($4^z$ tiles); for each $f \in F$ its geometry $g_f$ and the set $\tau(f,z) \subseteq T_z$ of tiles it intersects; per-piece encoded size $b_f(z,t)$ (clipped geometry + properties) and fixed per-tile overhead $h$ (Arrow IPC + zstd frame); a client contract in which a query at zoom $z$ fetches tiles of $T_z$ and may fall back to ancestors up to depth $d$ (relation $\mathrm{anc}_d(t)$: the tile plus its $\le d$ parents).

Decision variables: $x_{f,z,t} \in \{0,1\}$ — store a (clipped) piece of $f$ in tile $(z,t)$, for $t \in \bigcup_{z' \le z} \tau(f,z')$; $y_{z,t} \in \{0,1\}$ — tile $(z,t)$ exists, with $y_{z,t} \ge x_{f,z,t}$ for all $f$.

$$\min_{x,y} \; \sum_{z \in Z} \sum_{t \in T_z} \Big( h\, y_{z,t} + \sum_{f} x_{f,z,t}\, b_f(z,t) \Big)$$

subject to completeness under fallback: for every zoom $z \in Z$, every tile $t \in T_z$, and every feature $f$ with $g_f \cap \mathrm{extent}(t) \ne \emptyset$ (and $z$ inside $f$'s declared zoom band),

$$\sum_{(z',t') \in \mathrm{anc}_d(t)} x_{f,z',t'} \;\ge\; 1;$$

geometric validity: a stored piece in $(z,t)$ must cover $g_f \cap \mathrm{extent}(t)$ (clip semantics); and the project's no-thinning rule: features are never silently dropped unless an explicit budget is opted into (see §4). Optionally a secondary objective bounds per-query bytes: $\max_{t} \sum_f x_{f,z,t}\, b_f(z,t) \le B$.

**Incumbent.** The trivial feasible point of maximum replication: $x_{f,z,t} = 1$ for every $t \in \tau(f,z)$ at every in-band zoom (tiler.rs:1329-1362; polygon sweep tiler.rs:815-1010; line supercover clip.rs:719-947). Pruning is greedy and local only — the `min_features_per_tile` writer gate (tiler.rs:619-623) drops whole tiles post-hoc, and the zoom-band fields delegate the per-feature zoom decision to the data producer (tiler.rs:714-726). No cross-zoom deduplication, no overzoom-from-parent storage mode, no optimizer trades $h$-dominated tail tiles against fallback latency.

**Class.** Set multicover / facility location with a hierarchy (choosing which level stores each object); redundancy allocation in hierarchical caches; tippecanoe's `--drop-*`/coalesce family attacks the same tension heuristically. The general ILP is NP-hard; the incumbent is the maximal feasible point.

**Knobs.** `min_zoom`/`max_zoom` (defaults 0/14, tiler.rs:182-183; auto-recommended when unset — see §12); `min_features_per_tile` (default 1 = write every non-empty tile, tiler.rs:193); `min_zoom_field`/`max_zoom_field` (default None, tiler.rs:148-156); `clip_trajectories` (default true); `clip_min_vertices` (default 2).

**Gap.** A solver could exploit parent fallback systematically — store the long tail only at coarse zooms and let deep tiles overzoom, provably cutting $\sum h\, y_{z,t}$ (project memory notes single-feature deep-zoom tiles where frame overhead dominates payload). Measurable objective: total archive bytes at equal rendered completeness; secondary: tile count. PLANNED / counted-out with a recorded trigger: the additive **home-zoom decomposition** — each feature stored at exactly one deterministic home zoom $z^*(f)$, reader unions $[z_{min}..z]$ — is the $O(N)$ corner of the same feasible region whose incumbent is the $O(|Z| \times N)$ corner (earthquakes: 47.5 K quakes × 11 zooms = 523 K stored rows). It requires a deterministic (hash/rank-keyed, never RNG) home-zoom metric to satisfy the byte-reproducibility constraint; reader-side `lodMode:'additive'` already exists but is used only by AV LiDAR bundles built by scripts — a distinct, implemented additive partition problem (see §4) — and the build-side assignment does not exist. Trigger: per-zoom duplication dominating a dataset that matters. Cross-zoom geometry-blob sharing (flagged as the real win in the deltas research memo) fits the same ILP with shared-blob variables (see §6 — content-addressed dedup).

### 1.2 Clip-buffer sizing at tile boundaries (seam integrity vs. duplication)

**Setting.** Lines are clipped against tile rects expanded by `buffer_degrees = 0.001°` (~100 m at the equator) so strokes overlap the seam and joins/caps render without gaps (ClipConfig default clip.rs:87-104; Liang–Barsky against the buffered rect, clip.rs:340-385, 876-894). Polygons are clipped against the exact rect (`polygon_buffer_degrees = 0.0`): adjacent tiles cut the same ring at the same edge with identical arithmetic, emitting bit-identical seam vertices so fills rasterize watertight — any buffered strip would double-blend under `opacity < 1` because no renderer masks tiles at draw time (clip.rs:58-66, 92-94; same rect in the containment fast path, tiler.rs:998-1008). The vertex-on-boundary-counts-inside rule in Sutherland–Hodgman plus the world-anchored quantization grid preserve seam identity across tiles (clip.rs:1000-1003). Each unit of buffer duplicates a strip of geometry into the neighbouring tile, so the buffer is a pure duplication-vs-artifact knob — currently two constants in degrees, independent of zoom and latitude.

**Problem.** Given: zoom $z$ with tile width $W(z) = 360°/2^z$ in longitude; a rendered stroke width $\sigma$ px and tile pixel extent $E$ (one pixel $\approx W(z)/E$ degrees); dataset line geometry with boundary-crossing length $\Lambda(z)$ (degrees of path within a buffer strip per unit buffer, similarly $\Lambda_{poly}(z)$ for rings); per-vertex storage cost $\beta$; an artifact penalty $G(b)$ = expected visible seam defects (stroke gaps, clipped joins) at overlap $b$ degrees; and for fills a double-blend penalty $D(b)$ with $D(b) = 0 \iff b = 0$ under translucency.

Decision variables: $b_{line}(z) \ge 0$ and $b_{poly}(z) \ge 0$ (degrees of buffer, per zoom and geometry class).

$$\min_{b_{line}, b_{poly}} \; \sum_z \Big[ \beta\, \Lambda(z)\, b_{line}(z) + \alpha\, G\big(b_{line}(z)\big) \Big] + \sum_z \Big[ \beta\, \Lambda_{poly}(z)\, b_{poly}(z) + \alpha\, D\big(b_{poly}(z)\big) \Big]$$

subject to stroke continuity, $b_{line}(z) \ge \tfrac{\sigma}{2} \cdot \tfrac{W(z)}{E}$ (overlap at least the stroke's ground half-width so joins complete); the watertight-fill constraint $D(b_{poly}) = 0 \Rightarrow b_{poly}(z) = 0$ with bit-identical seam vertices (adjacent tiles must compute identical intersection parameters $t$ on the shared edge); and the ordering constraint that clipping run before simplification and after the antimeridian split (clip.rs:1300-1306).

**Incumbent.** Two hardcoded constants: $b_{line} = 0.001°$ at every zoom and latitude, $b_{poly} = 0$ (clip.rs:87-104; wired in `clip_config_from`, tiler.rs:665-683 — not CLI-exposed). The polygon side is arguably optimal: the constraint pins it at 0 and the bit-identical-seam construction achieves it (clip.rs:58-66). The line side never computes the constraint-satisfying minimum $b_{line}(z) \propto 2^{-z}$: its ground overlap is ~111 m regardless of zoom — a negligible $4\times10^{-5}$ of tile width at z4, but ~4.5 % of tile width at z14 (duplication larger than any plausible stroke needs), and its E–W ground size shrinks by $\cos\varphi$ at high latitude.

**Class.** Guard-band / halo sizing in domain decomposition and stencil computation (overlap vs. redundant compute); MVT's tippecanoe `--buffer` is the same knob expressed per-zoom in tile-relative units — i.e. already the $\propto 2^{-z}$ solution. Deterministic, offline, per-zoom separable; solvable in closed form once the constraint is stated.

**Knobs.** `buffer_degrees = 0.001` (clip.rs:92); `polygon_buffer_degrees = 0.0` (clip.rs:94); both compile-time constants in `clip_config_from` (tiler.rs:665-683).

**Gap.** Expressing the line buffer in tile-relative or pixel units (constant × $W(z)/E$) satisfies the continuity constraint everywhere while cutting deep-zoom duplication ~10–100×; measurable objective: duplicated vertex bytes at deep zooms with zero new seam artifacts. A per-dataset $\sigma$ (max stroke width from style hints) would tighten it further. Not implemented — PLANNED-shaped but not on any roadmap in the source notes.

### 1.3 Per-zoom geometry simplification (constrained distortion minimization, spatial and time-aware)

**Setting.** Below `simplify_max_zoom`, line geometry is simplified per (feature, zoom) before clipping: plain spatial Visvalingam–Whyatt (`simplify_vw`, simplify.rs:105-159) or, with `time_aware_simplify`, TD-TR using Synchronized Euclidean Distance — a vertex is kept when the object is far from where a constant-velocity mover would be at that vertex's own time, preserving pauses and speed changes that spatial simplifiers destroy (simplify.rs:364-439). Per-tile polygons get a topology-preserving VW variant after the clip, sharing one R\*-tree across exterior and holes, with hard guards: never below a valid closed ring, winding preserved, invalid exterior ⇒ whole polygon kept verbatim (simplify.rs:218-292; ordering tiler.rs:857-917). Tolerance comes from a legacy hardcoded per-zoom degree table (`calculate_epsilon`, simplify.rs:442-454) or the opt-in metric mode: 1.0 px of the zoom's ground resolution in a $\cos\varphi$-scaled locally-isometric space, so the same ground shape simplifies identically at every latitude (simplify.rs:30-70). Spatial VW that changes the vertex count forfeits supplied per-vertex times/values (recomputed by distance); TD-TR keeps real times because it selects a subset (clip.rs:789-827).

**Problem.** Given: a polyline $P = (v_1, \dots, v_n)$, $v_i = (x_i, y_i)$ (lon, lat), optional per-vertex times $t_i$; a zoom $z$ with tolerance $\varepsilon(z)$; latitude scale $s_\varphi = \max(\cos\bar\varphi, 0.05)$ making the working space locally metric ($x' = s_\varphi x$).

Decision variable: an index subset $K \subseteq \{1..n\}$ with $1, n \in K$ (kept vertices, order preserved) — for polygons, one subset per ring with closure and validity side-constraints.

$$\min_{K} \; |K| \quad \text{subject to} \quad \mathrm{dist}(P, P_K) \le \varepsilon(z)$$

where for the spatial problem $\mathrm{dist}$ is a shape deviation (Hausdorff-like; VW uses the effective-area proxy: every dropped vertex's triangle area $\le \varepsilon_{area}(z) = \varepsilon(z)^2$), and for the time-aware problem $\mathrm{dist}$ is the max Synchronized Euclidean Distance — for each dropped $v_i$ between kept anchors $v_a, v_b$:

$$\mathrm{SED}(i) = \Big\| v_i - \big(v_a + \tfrac{t_i - t_a}{t_b - t_a}(v_b - v_a)\big) \Big\|_{s_\varphi} \le \varepsilon(z).$$

Tolerance schedule: legacy $\varepsilon(z) \in \{0.01, 0.002, 0.0008, 0.0003, 0.0001, 0\}$ degrees by zoom band, or metric $\varepsilon(z) = 1.0\,\text{px} \cdot 156543\,\text{m} / (2^z \cdot 111320\,\text{m/deg})$. Side-constraints: (i) **lossless max tier** — for lines, $z > z_{simp}^{max} \Rightarrow K = \{1..n\}$ (simplify.rs:112); for polygons the gate is strict ($z \ge z_{simp}^{max}$ untouched, simplify.rs:185-189, 224-226) so the max-tiled-zoom fill tier stays bit-exact and watertight seams survive (pinned by test at simplify.rs:628-639); (ii) polygon topology — every output ring closed with $\ge 3$ distinct vertices, winding sign preserved, no self-intersection introduced, no ring ever dropped; (iii) subset-only selection (coordinates never synthesized); (iv) must run after the antimeridian split and after the per-tile clip for polygons (seam bit-identity, clip.rs:1300-1306); (v) supplied vertex-time arrays only survive when the vertex set is preserved.

**Incumbent.** Greedy heuristics for a problem with a known polynomial optimum: VW iteratively removes the min-effective-area vertex (geo crate, simplify.rs:139) and TD-TR is Douglas–Peucker-style recursive max-SED splitting (simplify.rs:393-439) — neither is minimum-cardinality for its distortion bound (Imai–Iri computes the true min-# in $O(n^2 \log n)$). The tolerance schedule is not optimized at all: the legacy table's degree values don't match their own resolution comments (simplify.rs:13-24) and yield up to ~2× inconsistent ground generalization at 60° latitude (test simplify.rs:734-773 proves degree mode latitude-sensitive, metric mode not); $\varepsilon$ is uniform per zoom rather than adapted to per-dataset stroke widths or density. Polygon acceptance is keep-original-on-failure (simplify.rs:265-291) — safe but suboptimal. Altitude on simplified line vertices is re-attached by nearest-segment interpolation (simplify.rs:457-502), a lossy heuristic outside the error bound.

**Class.** Min-# polyline simplification (Imai–Iri, exact in $O(n^2)$–$O(n^2\log n)$ under tolerance-band error) vs. the Douglas–Peucker/Visvalingam greedy family; trajectory-compression literature (TD-TR, SQUISH, direction-preserving) for the SED variant; rate–distortion theory for the $\varepsilon(z)$ schedule; topological constraints are where hardness appears for chains sharing topology. Offline, deterministic, per-feature separable.

**Knobs.** `simplify` (default false), `simplify_max_zoom` (default 14), `time_aware_simplify` (default false), `simplify_metric` (default false) (tiler.rs:188-191, 129-135); `TOLERANCE_PIXELS = 1.0` (simplify.rs:40); `MIN_COS_LAT = 0.05` (simplify.rs:45); legacy degree table (simplify.rs:442-454).

**Gap.** Optimal Imai–Iri-style selection under the same $\varepsilon(z)$ would strictly reduce vertex counts vs. greedy VW/TD-TR at identical guaranteed distortion — measurable as vertices/tile and tile bytes at each $z$ below the max tier. Choosing $\varepsilon(z)$ per dataset by minimizing bytes subject to a rendered-error budget (px), rather than fixing 1.0 px universally, is a 1D search the advisors never run; TD-TR's recursion is $O(n^2)$ worst-case where the DP gives guarantees. DEFERRED: making metric mode the default awaits a fleet republish, since it changes archive bytes (tiler.rs:130-135 keeps existing builds byte-identical). A 3D SED bringing the altitude re-interpolation inside the error metric is open for volumetric trajectories.

### 1.4 Parallel encode batch sizing under a writer memory budget (deterministic-order pipeline scheduling)

**Setting.** `encode_and_add_tiles` (tiler.rs:1685-1739) is the shared engine behind every pack-writer loop: it encodes tile payloads in parallel on the rayon pool but must hand them to storage in exactly the given sequential order — the byte-reproducibility contract (TileWriter doc tiler.rs:52-56, pinned by the `parallel_encode_writes_byte_identical_dataset` test, tiler.rs:2156-2234). Without a budget it encodes fixed chunks of `ENCODE_CHUNK = 1024` tiles (tiler.rs:1660-1663). With `--pack-memory-budget M`, transient encoded payloads (invisible to the writer's own buffer budget) are bounded by cutting sub-batches on a running encoded-byte cap of $M/4$, growing one worker-wave at a time so parallelism never collapses, and flushing each sub-batch in order before encoding the next (tiler.rs:1690-1736). Batch boundaries cannot change output bytes — only throughput and peak RSS.

**Problem.** Given: an ordered tile sequence $1..N$ with payload sizes $e_i$ unknown until encoded (encoding cost $c_i$ CPU-time); $W$ workers; a memory budget $M$ for the writer, of which transient encoded-but-unflushed payloads may use at most $\approx \kappa M$ with $\kappa = 1/4$; the hard constraint that `add_tile_full` is called in index order.

Decision variables: batch boundaries $0 = n_0 < n_1 < \dots < n_k = N$, each batch $(n_{j-1}, n_j]$ encoded in parallel then flushed sequentially.

$$\min_{\{n_j\}} \; \underbrace{\sum_j \Big( \max_{\text{workers}} \text{encode time of batch } j + \text{flush time of batch } j \Big)}_{\text{makespan}} \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \max_j \sum_{i \in \text{batch } j} e_i \le \kappa M \; (+\text{one-wave overshoot}), \quad \text{flush order} = \text{index order}$$

The online difficulty: $e_i$ is revealed only after encoding tile $i$, so batch cutting is an online stopping rule; utilization requires each batch $\ge W$ tiles.

**Incumbent.** Online greedy wave-growth: encode `workers` tiles at a time, accumulate until the running encoded-byte total reaches $M/4$ (always at least one wave), flush, repeat (tiler.rs:1707-1736). The $1/4$ fraction and one-wave granularity are constants, not derived from actual encode/flush overlap; there is no encode/flush pipelining — flush is serial dead time for the pool between sub-batches — and no size prediction to pre-cut batches.

**Class.** Bounded-buffer producer–consumer / pipeline scheduling with in-order commit (out-of-order execution with a byte-sized reorder buffer); online batching with unknown item sizes (one-way-trading / ski-rental-flavored stopping rules). Online, stochastic in $e_i$.

**Knobs.** `ENCODE_CHUNK = 1024` (tiler.rs:1663); byte cap $= \max(\text{budget}/4, 1)$ (tiler.rs:1692-1696); wave $=$ `rayon::current_num_threads()` under a budget, else the whole chunk (tiler.rs:1700-1704); `--pack-memory-budget` and `--workers` at the CLI.

**Gap.** Overlapping the sequential flush of batch $j$ with the parallel encode of batch $j{+}1$ (double-buffered halves of the byte cap) would remove the flush bubble at identical memory — measurable as wall-clock build time at fixed peak RSS on large national builds (e.g. the 9.5 h storm-3d run). A size predictor (per-tile feature-count model, as `tile_feature_size` already estimates for the §4 budget) could pre-cut batches without the one-wave overshoot. Both PLANNED-shaped; neither implemented.

## 2. The temporal axis: buckets, precision, and the time-LOD pyramid

Where §1 partitions space, this subsystem partitions and encodes _time_: every tile is addressed by a temporal bucket, every feature carries an interval, and every trajectory vertex carries a timestamp. The decisions here — bucket width and phase, interval placement, adaptive vs. fixed windowing, coarse-tier materialization, and the bit-level encoding of time columns — jointly trade fetch granularity, directory size, storage duplication, playback smoothness, and payload bytes. All of them are set at build time against a playback workload that is only observed at read time, which is the recurring source of hardness.

### 2.1 Temporal bucket-width selection

**Setting.** Every STT tile is addressed $(z, x, y, \text{bucket})$ with $\text{bucket} = \lfloor t/\Delta \rfloor \cdot \Delta$ on an epoch-anchored fixed-width grid (docs/spec/time-model.md §3). $\Delta$ is the project's central temporal knob: it simultaneously sets client fetch granularity (the prefetcher enumerates exactly these boundaries ahead of the playhead, spec §3.2 — see §9), directory entry count, per-tile payload size, scrub granularity, and — for trajectories — geometry duplication, since every clipped segment is sliced at each bucket boundary it crosses with an interpolated shared vertex (`slice_segment_temporally`, crates/stt-build/src/clip.rs:585-701). The builder default is 1 h (crates/stt-build/src/tiler.rs:185; crates/stt-core/src/metadata.rs:312); `stt-optimize` recommends a value via `recommend_bucket_size` (crates/stt-optimize/src/analysis/temporal.rs:310-373), emitted as the `--temporal-bucket` flag (crates/stt-optimize/src/recommend.rs:57). The grid phase is fixed at 0 (epoch anchor) by the spec and never optimized.

**Problem.** Given: dataset span $T = T_1 - T_0$ (ms); feature start times $t_1,\dots,t_N$ with $U = |\{t_i\}|$ distinct values; for each zoom $z$ and spatial cell $c$ the feature multiset $F_{z,c}$ produced by spatial tiling (§1); trajectory edge set $E$ of consecutive vertex-time pairs $(t^-_e, t^+_e)$; a workload distribution $\mathcal{W}$ over playback sessions $w$ (viewport cells $V_w(z)$, playhead trajectory, speed $v_w$ in sim-ms/wall-ms, network latency $\ell$); cost constants $c_{req}$ (per tile fetch), $c_{dir}$ (index bytes per directory entry), $c_{dup}$ (bytes per duplicated boundary vertex + per-slice property copy); target loop length $S$ seconds at frame rate $f$.

Decision variables: bucket width $\Delta \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0}$ (ms) and phase $\phi \in [0,\Delta) \cap \mathbb{Z}$, defining the bucket map $b_{\Delta,\phi}(t) = \lfloor (t-\phi)/\Delta \rfloor$.

Derived quantities: tile set $\mathcal{T}(\Delta,\phi) = \{(z,c,k) : \exists i \in F_{z,c},\ b_{\Delta,\phi}(t_i)=k\}$; per-session request count $R_w(\Delta) \approx \sum_{\text{runway advances}} |V_w(z)| \cdot \lceil L_w/\Delta \rceil$ where $L_w$ is the buffered time-window length; wasted bytes $B^{waste}_w(\Delta)$ = bytes of fetched features never intersecting a rendered window (grows with $\Delta$); stall risk $P_{stall,w}(\Delta) = \Pr[\text{playhead reaches an unbuffered boundary before its fetch completes}]$, increasing in $v_w \ell / \Delta$ burstiness; slicing overhead $X(\Delta,\phi) = \sum_{e \in E} \#\{k : t^-_e < k\Delta + \phi < t^+_e\}$.

$$\min_{\Delta,\phi}\; \mathbb{E}_{w\sim\mathcal{W}}\Big[ c_{req}\,R_w(\Delta) + B^{waste}_w(\Delta) + \lambda_{stall} P_{stall,w}(\Delta)\Big] \;+\; c_{dir}\,|\mathcal{T}(\Delta,\phi)| \;+\; c_{dup}\,X(\Delta,\phi) \;+\; \lambda_{loop}\Big|\tfrac{T}{\Delta} - S f\Big|$$

$$\text{s.t.}\quad \Delta > 0 \ (\text{spec MUST}),\qquad \tfrac{T}{\Delta} \le U \ (\text{no finer than timestamp resolution}),\qquad \tfrac{N}{T/\Delta} \ge m_{min} \ (\text{avg features/bucket floor}).$$

The last objective term encodes playback smoothness: a loop of $\approx S f$ addressable frames is the QoS target the incumbent bakes in. The incumbent restricts $\Delta$ to a 14-value ladder $\mathcal{L} = \{1\text{s}, 1\text{m}, 5\text{m}, 10\text{m}, 15\text{m}, 30\text{m}, 1\text{h}, 2\text{h}, 4\text{h}, 6\text{h}, 12\text{h}, 1\text{d}, 1\text{w}, 30\text{d}\}$ and fixes $\phi = 0$ — epoch anchoring buys cross-dataset grid compatibility and exact nesting of LOD multiples, at the cost of possibly splitting a natural burst across two buckets.

**Incumbent.** `recommend_bucket_size` (crates/stt-optimize/src/analysis/temporal.rs:310-373): target bucket count $= \mathrm{round}(45 \times 30 \times \text{factor})$ with factor $\in \{1.5\ \text{bursty}, 0.5\ \text{sparse}, 1\ \text{else}\}$ (temporal.rs:341-346), clamped by $\min(U, N/10)$ (temporal.rs:351-357), then a greedy scan of the hardcoded ladder returning the _finest_ width with $T/\Delta \le$ target (temporal.rs:361-366), with coarsest-entry fallback (temporal.rs:371-372). The builder default is a constant 1 h (tiler.rs:185). Hardcoded/greedy aspects: the single derived target ignores request cost, wasted bytes, stall risk, and slicing duplication entirely; the constants 45 s / 30 fps / 10 features / ×1.5 / ×0.5 are unfitted; phase is fixed; the ladder omits e.g. 3 h/8 h. For a known workload the optimum is computable by direct enumeration over $\Delta$ (the objective is piecewise monotone between ladder points), which the incumbent never attempts even though stt-optimize measures density per zoom.

**Class.** Histogram bin-width selection (a Freedman–Diaconis analog with a workload term); uniform scalar quantizer / paging block-size design; storage-system fetch-granularity selection. Offline and single-parameter, so exactly solvable by enumeration _given_ a workload model — the hardness is stochastic (the playback workload is unobserved at build time), making this decision-under-uncertainty / newsvendor-flavored rather than combinatorial.

**Knobs.** `--temporal-bucket <dur>` (default 1 h; `parse_duration` accepts ms/s/m/h/d, fractional); `TARGET_PLAYBACK_SECONDS = 45.0`; `PLAYBACK_FPS = 30.0`; `MIN_FEATURES_PER_BUCKET = 10`; `frame_factor` ∈ {1.5, 0.5, 1.0}.

**Gap.** A workload-aware solver could evaluate the full objective per ladder value using the density model stt-optimize already computes (density.rs:121 estimates tile counts from `recommended_bucket_ms`) plus a session simulator, measurably reducing fetches/session and stall probability at equal archive size. Phase optimization ($\phi \ne 0$) could align bucket edges to burst gaps, reducing slicing duplication $X$ and per-tile skew — measurable as byte and tile-count deltas on bursty datasets. Neither is implemented or planned.

### 2.2 Interval-feature placement and covering bounds

**Setting.** A feature valid over $[s_i, e_i]$ is stored in exactly _one_ base-tier tile — the bucket containing $s_i$ (`chunk_by_temporal_bucket`, crates/stt-build/src/tiler.rs:1444-1456; normative in spec §3.1) — never duplicated into the other buckets its interval overlaps. Findability is restored at read time by two exact per-tile bounds computed at build: `time_end` = max feature end time (tiler.rs:1422-1426; spec §5 makes the _tight_ bound load-bearing for correctness) and `cover_t_min` = min start time over kept features (tiler.rs:1564-1577). The reader keeps a tile for window $[w_s, w_e]$ iff `time_end` ≥ $w_s$ and (`cover_t_min` ?? `time_start`) ≤ $w_e$ (spec §5); the paged directory lifts the same bounds to per-leaf $[t_{min}, t_{max}]$ descriptors for page-level pruning (see §7). Trajectories soften the cost: fixed-grid builds temporally slice each clipped segment at every bucket boundary (clip.rs:936-941, clip.rs:593-701), so long paths are physically partitioned — point/polygon interval features are not.

**Problem.** Given: features $i = 1..N$ with intervals $[s_i, e_i]$, payload bytes $\beta_i$, assigned spatial cell(s); a bucket grid of width $\Delta$ indexed by $k$; a workload $\mathcal{Q}$ of temporal query windows $W = [w_s, w_e]$ per cell; and the reader's fixed prune predicate: a tile $\tau$ with stored bounds $(\underline{t}_\tau, \overline{t}_\tau)$ is fetched iff $\overline{t}_\tau \ge w_s \wedge \underline{t}_\tau \le w_e$.

Decision variables: for each feature a nonempty set of holding buckets $A_i \subseteq \{k : [k\Delta, (k{+}1)\Delta) \cap [s_i, e_i] \ne \emptyset\}$ (physical copies); per-tile bounds $\underline{t}_\tau \le \min_{i \in \tau} s_i$, $\overline{t}_\tau \ge \max_{i \in \tau} e_i$, restricted to a copy's local validity when features are split.

Correctness constraint: for every window $W$ and every feature with $[s_i,e_i] \cap W \ne \emptyset$, at least one bucket in $A_i$ must be fetched under the prune predicate (no silently lost intervals).

$$\min_{\{A_i\}, \{(\underline{t},\overline{t})\}} \;\; \underbrace{\sum_i |A_i|\,\beta_i}_{\text{storage + dedup-lost bytes}} \;+\; \lambda\, \mathbb{E}_{W \sim \mathcal{Q}}\Big[ \underbrace{\sum_{\tau\ \text{fetched}} \text{bytes}(\tau) \;-\; \sum_{i : [s_i,e_i] \cap W \ne \emptyset} \beta_i}_{\text{read amplification (false-positive bytes)}} \Big]$$

Single placement ($|A_i| = 1$ at $b(s_i)$) minimizes the first term but forces $\overline{t}_\tau$ up to the longest resident interval, so every later window fetches the whole start-bucket tile including its unrelated features; full replication ($A_i$ = all overlapped buckets) zeroes false positives from long-lived features but multiplies storage by mean interval length $/\Delta$. The spec currently hard-constrains $A_i = \{\lfloor s_i/\Delta \rfloor\}$ (MUST NOT duplicate, spec §8) and mandates the tight `time_end`.

**Incumbent.** The zero-replication extreme with exact bounds: `chunk_by_temporal_bucket` floors every feature to its start bucket (tiler.rs:1450-1453); `time_end` is the exact max end (tiler.rs:1422-1426) and `cover_t_min` the exact min start over kept features (tiler.rs:1564-1572); readers prune with those bounds (docs/spec/time-model.md:239-251). Within the fixed one-bound-per-tile family the computed bounds are optimal (tightest possible). What is fixed rather than optimized: the replication degree itself (frozen at 1 by spec), and the absence of any long-lived-feature segregation — one decade-long interval in a busy tile makes that entire tile a permanent fetch for every subsequent window, dragging all co-resident short features with it. Trajectories escape via boundary slicing (clip.rs:593-701); points and polygons do not.

**Class.** Interval stabbing / temporal-database partitioning (start-time vs. overlap placement is the classic event-table vs. snapshot tradeoff); the spatial analog is single-placement vs. per-tile duplication in vector-tile clipping (STT duplicates spatially via clipping but not temporally). With a query distribution and per-feature replication choice this is replication-placement / facility-location (NP-hard in general); the per-tile-bounds subproblem is trivially optimal per tile; partitioning features into "short" and "long-lived" tiles maps to weighted interval clustering, polynomial in 1-D per cell.

**Knobs.** None directly — the placement rule is normative. Indirect: $\Delta$ (§2.1) scales the read-amplification term; `--adaptive-temporal` replaces the grid entirely (§2.3).

**Gap.** A principled variant would split each tile's features by residual lifetime — a per-bucket "long-lived" sibling tile, or bounded replication for intervals spanning $> K$ buckets — cutting expected false-positive bytes on heavy-tailed-duration datasets (trips, storm cells) at a measurable storage cost; the objective moved is bytes-fetched-per-query-window at fixed archive size. Requires a spec erratum (the no-duplication MUST) or the declared-variant mechanism. Not implemented, not planned.

### 2.3 Adaptive temporal windowing: equal-mass 1-D partitioning for bursty data

**Setting.** With `--adaptive-temporal N`, fixed-grid chunking is replaced per spatial cell by `chunk_adaptive_by_count` (tiler.rs:1465-1489): features are sorted by timestamp and greedily packed into contiguous windows of ~$N$ features; a window closes once it holds ≥ $N$ features _and_ the next feature opens a distinct timestamp (features sharing one exact timestamp are inseparable — the window key, its first feature's timestamp, becomes the tile's $t$ address, and two windows may not collide on $(z,x,y,t)$). Dense bursts get many fine windows, sparse gaps few coarse ones; no feature is dropped. Fixed-bucket trajectory slicing is disabled (no grid to slice against, tiler.rs:670-677). Caveat carried from the summary-tier analysis: this applies to the _in-memory_ build path only — the streaming build path keeps fixed buckets (tiler.rs:136-141). The advisor recommends the mode only for Bursty timelines (events/day CoV > 1.5, analysis/temporal.rs:270) with max/median features-per-tile skew ≥ 10× at the recommended max zoom, choosing $N = \mathrm{round\_nice}(4 \times \text{median features/tile})$ clamped to $[1000, 50000]$ (advisors/temporal.rs:136-176).

**Problem.** Given: one spatial cell's features sorted by time $t_{(1)} \le \dots \le t_{(n)}$ with payload bytes $\beta_{(j)}$; the equivalence partition $G_1, \dots, G_m$ of features by exact timestamp (atoms that cannot be split); a target window mass $N^*$ (or, more fundamentally, the tile-size band $[\underline{B}, \overline{B}]$ the format wants per tile).

Decision variables: window count $K \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0}$ and breakpoints $0 = j_0 < j_1 < \dots < j_K = m$ over atoms, defining windows $W_k = G_{j_{k-1}+1} \cup \dots \cup G_{j_k}$ with key $\kappa_k = \min\{t : t \in W_k\}$.

$$\min_{K, \{j_k\}} \;\; \max_k \; \text{bytes}(W_k) \qquad\text{or}\qquad \min_{K,\{j_k\}} \sum_k \big(|W_k| - N^*\big)^2$$

$$\text{s.t.}\quad \kappa_1 < \kappa_2 < \dots < \kappa_K \ \text{(distinct } (z,x,y,t) \text{ keys — automatic for atom-respecting contiguous windows)},$$

$$\text{each atom } G_g \text{ lies wholly inside one } W_k \ \text{(atoms unsplittable)},\qquad \text{no feature dropped (losslessness)}.$$

The meta-problem (choosing $N^*$): given per-zoom density statistics (median $\tilde{m}$, max $M$ features/tile), choose $N^*$ minimizing expected deviation of realized tile sizes from the format's efficient band, subject to $N^* \in [N_{min}, N_{max}]$; the advisor's incumbent is the closed form $N^* = \mathrm{nice}(4\tilde{m})$ clamped to $[1000, 50000]$, gated on $\mathrm{CoV} > 1.5 \wedge M/\tilde{m} \ge 10$.

**Incumbent.** Greedy first-fit sweep (tiler.rs:1465-1489): open a window, close it at the first atom boundary at-or-after $N$ features, key it by first timestamp. Windows systematically overshoot $N$ (close at ≥ $N$ plus the whole final atom) and the last window can be arbitrarily small — no lookahead, no balancing; the min-max or min-variance optimum is solvable exactly by DP in $O(mK)$ or by parametric search in near-linear time, which the code does not attempt. The advisor's $N^*$ (advisors/temporal.rs:150) is a hardcoded multiplier with no feedback from realized tile bytes. Deeper structural gap: window boundaries are independent per cell and keyed by data timestamps, so the client-side contract of enumerable grid boundaries (spec §3.2) no longer holds — adjacent cells fragment at unrelated instants, hurting cross-cell fetch batching; boundary alignment across cells is absent from the objective (and memory notes adaptive mode silently breaks fixed-boundary prefetch enumeration).

**Class.** Linear (contiguous) array partitioning / painter's-partition — exact DP $O(mK)$, greedy is a constant-factor approximation; equal-frequency and V-optimal histogram construction (V-optimal DP-solvable); adaptive binning in time-series stores. Offline and deterministic, so exact optimization is entirely feasible; the cross-cell alignment variant becomes a joint partitioning problem closer to graph partitioning.

**Knobs.** `--adaptive-temporal <N>` (no default; advisor suggests $\mathrm{nice}(4\times\text{median})$ in $[1000, 50000]$); `ADAPTIVE_SKEW_THRESHOLD = 10.0`; `ADAPTIVE_SKEW_CONFIDENT = 20.0`; CoV burst gate = 1.5; `ADAPTIVE_N_MIN/MAX = 1000/50000`.

**Gap.** Replace the greedy with the exact min-max DP per cell (cheap: cells are small) and add a cross-cell alignment term — snap boundaries to a shared candidate set (e.g. quantiles of the cell-union distribution) so neighboring cells share fetch instants. Measurable objectives: variance of per-tile bytes, and requests-per-playhead-advance in multi-cell playback. Also fit the 4×median multiplier against realized tile-byte bands. None implemented or planned.

### 2.4 Temporal LOD pyramid design: tier widths and zoom cutoffs

**Setting.** For long timelines, stt-build emits coarser-bucket tiers alongside the base: each LOD level $(\text{bucket\_ms}, \text{max\_zoom\_level})$ produces one extra tile per (zoom ≤ cutoff, cell, coarse bucket) holding _exactly_ the union of the base features re-bucketed at the coarser width — no aggregation or thinning (spec §4; `emit_temporal_lod_tiles`, tiler.rs:376-421, reuses the base tiler with the bucket overridden and recursion disabled). Validation enforces strict ascending integer multiples of the base bucket (tiler.rs:426-453; metadata.rs:374-382). The reader opts in per zoom via a fixed rule: `temporal_lod_for_zoom` returns the coarsest level whose `max_zoom_level` ≥ zoom (metadata.rs:387-393), so zoomed-out decade scrubs read 30-day tiles instead of thousands of hourly ones (the reader-side tier request-granularity policy is treated separately — see §7). Tiers are additive: the archive grows by the duplicated coarse tiles.

**Problem.** Given: base bucket $\Delta$, span $T$, zoom range $[z_{min}, z_{max}]$; per-zoom base byte mass $b(z) = \sum_{\text{cells } c} \text{bytes}(F_{z,c})$ and nonempty-cell counts; a workload $\mathcal{Q}$ over queries $q = (z_q, V_q, L_q)$ (zoom, viewport cell count, time-window length); candidate tier widths $\mathcal{K} = \{k\Delta : k \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 2}\}$.

Decision variables: a tier set $\mathcal{J} = \{(\Delta_j, z_j)\}_{j=1}^J$ with $\Delta_j \in \mathcal{K}$, $\Delta_1 < \dots < \Delta_J$, cutoffs $z_j \in [z_{min}, z_{max}]$. The reader's selection rule is fixed: $\Delta_{used}(z) = \max\big(\{\Delta_j : z \le z_j\} \cup \{\Delta\}\big)$.

Request cost of a query: $R_q(\mathcal{J}) = V_q \cdot \lceil L_q / \Delta_{used}(z_q) \rceil$ fetches, each of expected size $\approx \frac{\Delta_{used}}{\Delta} \times$ (base tile bytes), since coarse tiles are unions. Added storage: $\text{cost}(\mathcal{J}) = \sum_j \sum_{z \le z_j} b(z)$ (every tier duplicates the full feature mass over its zoom range, modulo per-tile overhead savings).

$$\min_{\mathcal{J}} \; \mathbb{E}_{q \sim \mathcal{Q}}\big[ c_{req}\, R_q(\mathcal{J}) + c_{byte}\, B_q(\mathcal{J}) \big] \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \text{cost}(\mathcal{J}) \le B_{budget},$$

$$\Delta_j \equiv 0 \pmod{\Delta},\qquad \Delta_j > \Delta,\qquad \Delta_1 < \dots < \Delta_J \quad(\text{normative build invariants}),$$

where $B_q$ is bytes fetched — coarse tiles trade request count for bytes (the fetch is fatter but single).

**Incumbent.** The stt-optimize temporal advisor (this is where §12's recommend logic instantiates for the time axis): `advise_temporal_lod` + `pick_tiers` (crates/stt-optimize/src/advisors/temporal.rs:71-131) is a threshold rule with no measurement at all (advisors/temporal.rs:56-66) — fire iff $T/\Delta >$ `LOD_BUCKET_THRESHOLD` = 5000, then a greedy forward scan over the 5-entry hardcoded ladder `CLEAN_TIERS` = {1s, 1m, 1h, 1d, 30d} accepting the first ≤ 2 entries that divide the base evenly, are ≥ `TIER_STEP_FACTOR` = 4 × the previous level, and leave ≥ 2 buckets (pick_tiers, advisors/temporal.rs:114-131) — it minimizes neither scan cost nor growth, just takes the earliest feasible ladder entries. Zoom cutoffs are not optimized at all: `parse_temporal_lod` defaults every tier to the fallback max zoom unless the user writes `@z` (build_options.rs:58-83), so by default every coarse tier is emitted at _every_ zoom (maximal duplication) even though its benefit concentrates at low zooms. No workload model, byte budget, or $b(z)$ estimate enters the choice; the projected line only reports bucket-count ratios (advisors/temporal.rs:100-104). The interaction with §2.3 is unresolved: both LOD and adaptive fire on bursty long timelines, handled only by an appended "consider one or the other first" sentence (advisors/temporal.rs:57-65). The reader rule (coarsest ≤ cutoff, metadata.rs:387-393) is fixed and never co-optimized with tier spacing.

**Class.** Materialized-view selection in data cubes (Harinarayan–Rajaraman–Ullman): choosing which aggregation granularities to materialize under a space budget — NP-hard, greedy gives a $(1-1/e)$ approximation; also multi-level index / LSM fan-out selection and image-pyramid level spacing. Offline, deterministic; the candidate space is small enough for exact enumeration if a workload and $b(z)$ model existed.

**Knobs.** `--temporal-lod "<dur>[@zoom],..."` (e.g. `1d@8,30d@4`); `LOD_BUCKET_THRESHOLD = 5000` buckets; `TIER_STEP_FACTOR = 4`; max 2 advised tiers; `CLEAN_TIERS` ladder {1s, 1m, 1h, 1d, 30d}.

**Gap.** Optimize zoom cutoffs jointly with widths using the per-zoom byte mass stt-optimize already measures: emitting a 30 d tier only for $z \le 4$ instead of all zooms cuts the duplication term by the (dominant) high-zoom byte share with zero query-cost loss for the queries that would ever pick it — measurable as added-archive-bytes per unit of request-count reduction. A budgeted HRU-style greedy over (width, cutoff) pairs would strictly dominate the fixed 4×-step ladder pick. Reduced/aggregated tiers (content ≠ exact union) are an explicitly PLANNED declared variant (spec §4), not built — they would change the storage term from duplication to sublinear and reshape the whole problem.

### 2.5 Per-vertex time quantization: width-tiered delta coding under an error ceiling

**Setting.** Trajectory tiles carry a per-vertex timestamp column (`vertex_time`) so trips animate along their path. The encoder stores it as deltas against a per-layer origin with a per-layer step (absolute = origin + delta × step), trying three tiers narrowest-first (`build_vertex_time_array`, crates/stt-core/src/arrow_tile/columns.rs:393-483): `List<UInt16>` (2 B/vertex, exact-ms up to 65.5 s span), `List<UInt32>` (4 B/vertex, exact-ms up to 49.7 days), and absolute `List<Int64>` (8 B/vertex, exact, unbounded). For each width it computes the smallest step covering the layer span, $\text{step} = \max(1, \lceil \text{span} / \text{max\_delta} \rceil)$; the tier is accepted iff step ≤ the configured ceiling (default `DEFAULT_VERTEX_TIME_MAX_STEP_MS` = 1000 ms; floored to 1 in `EncoderSettings::resolve` so a 0 value cannot silently force the 4× Int64 fallback, build_options.rs:243-252). Exceeding the ceiling at every delta tier falls back to exact Int64 with a once-per-process warning — bounded loss or no loss, never a silent precision cliff. (origin, step) ride in TILE_META (encode.rs:724-726). This is the project's "byte-level time-binning" lever; the u32 tier exists because falling from u16 straight to Int64 cost 14.3% of nyc-taxi-flows tile bytes (columns.rs:382-388).

**Problem (rate minimization under bounded distortion).** Given: one layer's vertex time lists with $n_v$ total vertices, span $\sigma = \max_i t_i - \min_i t_i$ (ms); error ceiling $s_{max} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 1}$ (user precision, ms); width menu $\mathcal{W} = \{16, 32, 64\}$ bits with delta ceilings $D_w = 2^w - 1$ ($D_{64} = \infty$, exact).

Decision variables: width $w \in \mathcal{W}$; step $s \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 1}$ (meaningful only for $w < 64$); origin $o \in \mathbb{Z}$ (incumbent fixes $o = \min_i t_i$). Encoding: $\hat{t}_i = o + \lfloor (t_i - o)/s \rfloor \cdot s$.

$$\min_{w, s} \;\; \underbrace{n_v \cdot w / 8}_{\text{column bytes}} \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \underbrace{\max_i |t_i - \hat{t}_i| < s \le s_{max}}_{\text{distortion bound}}, \qquad \Big\lceil \frac{\sigma}{D_w} \Big\rceil \le s \;\; (w < 64) \;\;(\text{addressability}),$$

with the tie-break that among byte-minimal choices the smallest feasible $s$ is taken (maximal precision at equal rate). $w = 64$ is always feasible with zero distortion, so a solution always exists; the lossy tiers are chosen only when they fit the ceiling. The knob-level meta-problem: choose $s_{max}$ to minimize bytes subject to the reconstruction error being imperceptible at playback, $s_{max} \cdot \frac{f}{v} \le \epsilon_{frames}$, where $v$ is playback speed (sim-ms/wall-ms), $f$ frame rate, $\epsilon_{frames}$ tolerable per-vertex frame error — the incumbent hardcodes $s_{max} = 1000$ as "below anything playback can show".

**Incumbent.** Narrowest-first greedy scan (columns.rs:426-449): for $w = 16$ then $32$, compute the minimal feasible $s$; accept the first with $s \le s_{max}$; else exact Int64 (columns.rs:465-482). Deltas are computed and compared in u64 before narrowing (overflow-safe, columns.rs:420-437). For the lexicographic objective as stated, this greedy is _exactly optimal_ within the family {per-layer uniform quantizer, origin = min, width menu} — the gap is the family, not the search: (a) a single uniform step per layer wastes rate on layers whose vertex times cluster (delta-of-delta / Gorilla-style timestamp coding reaches ~1-2 B/vertex lossless on regular cadences, before zstd); (b) the objective is pre-zstd bytes, but zstd-19 downstream compresses low-entropy u16 streams differently than i64 — the true objective (post-compression bytes) is never measured; (c) $s_{max} = 1000$ ms is a global constant uncoupled from playback speed and bucket width (a 30-day LOD tile tolerates far coarser vertex time than a 1 h base tile at equal perceptual error); (d) per-layer origin is optimal only given uniform steps; (e) the menu has no intermediate tier — a 20-hour layer at a 1 s ceiling pays 4 B/vertex where ~17 bits suffice — and per-feature or bucketed origins would rescue wide temporal-LOD layers that today hit the exact-i64 fallback (2-4× larger, warned at columns.rs:453-462).

**Class.** Rate–distortion / uniform scalar quantizer design with a hard sup-norm distortion bound; timestamp compression in TSDBs (Gorilla delta-of-delta, Chimp); bit-width selection in lightweight columnar encodings. Deterministic, offline, per-layer independent — exactly solvable (and the incumbent is exact within its family); the open question is family enrichment and an end-to-end rate objective.

**Knobs.** `--vertex-time-precision <ms>` (default 1000 = `DEFAULT_VERTEX_TIME_MAX_STEP_MS`, columns.rs:196; clamped ≥ 1 at resolve, build_options.rs:249-252); `--no-compact-times` does _not_ affect this column (separate lever, §2.6).

**Gap.** Measure post-zstd bytes per tier choice and let the encoder pick per layer against compressed size (the true objective — see §5); add a delta-of-delta tier for regular-cadence trajectories (GPS pings, transit) targeting ~1 B/vertex lossless; scale $s_{max}$ with the tile's `temporal_bucket_ms` so LOD tiers quantize proportionally coarser at equal perceptual error. Objective moved: total pack bytes for trajectory-heavy archives at fixed playback fidelity. None implemented or planned.

### 2.6 Compact feature-time form selection: per-layer lossless code choice under a schema-template budget

**Setting.** Every feature carries `start_time`/`end_time` (i64 wire semantics). On the frame path the encoder chooses, per tile-layer, a compact lossless form per column (`choose_time_forms`, crates/stt-core/src/arrow_tile/encode.rs:189-231): `start_time` becomes UInt32 offsets from $t_0 = \min$ start iff every offset fits $[0, 2^{32})$, else absolute Int64; `end_time` is omitted entirely when every duration is zero (instant features — the reader synthesizes end = start), becomes UInt32 durations against each feature's _own_ start when they all fit (so a long-lived feature inside a wide tile still compacts), else absolute Int64. The wire discriminants ride in TILE_META (`StartTimeForm`/`EndTimeForm`, frame.rs:184-260), and decode re-inflates everything to the canonical absolute Int64 pair so downstream never branches (`reinflate_compact_times`, decode.rs:166-232). Empty layers are pinned to the absolute pair because a compact verdict over zero features would fork the layer's schema template — each distinct core shape costs a `manifest.schemas` entry (encode.rs:184-188). Rows are stable-sorted by `start_time` at encode so `TILE_META.sorted` can be declared (sort_rows_by_start_time, encode.rs:791-870; the residual row-permutation freedom is a §3 problem). The feature is on by default; `--no-compact-times` simultaneously suppresses the encoding and the dataset-level `time-delta` capability declaration so the two can never disagree (build_options.rs:253, 284-291). This halves-or-better the two hottest per-feature columns (8+8 B → 4+4, 4+0, or 0 extra for instants).

**Problem.** Given: a dataset of tile-layers $\ell = 1..L$, each with feature count $n_\ell$, start times $s_{\ell i}$, end times $e_{\ell i}$; a per-layer anchor $t_{0\ell} = \min_i s_{\ell i}$; candidate start-forms $\mathcal{F}_s = \{\text{I64}, \text{U32off}\}$ and end-forms $\mathcal{F}_e = \{\text{I64}, \text{Dur32}, \text{Zero}\}$ with per-feature byte costs $c(\text{I64}) = 8$, $c(\text{U32off}) = c(\text{Dur32}) = 4$, $c(\text{Zero}) = 0$; a schema-template cost $c_{tmpl}$ per distinct core column shape in `manifest.schemas`.

Decision variables: per-layer forms $(f^s_\ell, f^e_\ell) \in \mathcal{F}_s \times \mathcal{F}_e$.

Feasibility (losslessness — exact round-trip):

$$f^s_\ell = \text{U32off} \Rightarrow \forall i:\, 0 \le s_{\ell i} - t_{0\ell} \le 2^{32}{-}1; \quad f^e_\ell = \text{Dur32} \Rightarrow \forall i:\, 0 \le e_{\ell i} - s_{\ell i} \le 2^{32}{-}1; \quad f^e_\ell = \text{Zero} \Rightarrow \forall i:\, e_{\ell i} = s_{\ell i}.$$

$$\min_{\{(f^s_\ell, f^e_\ell)\}} \;\; \sum_\ell n_\ell \big( c(f^s_\ell) + c(f^e_\ell) \big) \; + \; c_{tmpl} \cdot \big| \{ \text{distinct core shapes induced by } (f^s_\ell, f^e_\ell, \dots) \} \big|$$

The second term couples otherwise-independent per-layer choices: a rare shape (e.g. a compact verdict over an empty layer) buys nothing in the first term while adding a whole template — exactly the case the incumbent special-cases.

**Incumbent.** Feasibility-gated maximal compaction per layer (encode.rs:189-231, applied at 260-303): always compact when the range checks pass, preferring Zero over Dur32 over I64 for the end column, with `checked_sub` against i64 extremes so near-overflow layers fall back rather than wrap (encode.rs:197-199); empty layers pinned to absolute (encode.rs:184-188) — a hand-rolled fix for the template-coupling term's only observed pathological case. Within the fixed two-menu family this greedy is optimal per layer (4 < 8, 0 < 4, feasibility monotone). What is constant rather than optimized: the menu itself (no U16 tier — offsets against the _bucket_ start rather than min-feature start would fit u16 for fine-bucket archives, halving again); the anchor is fixed at min-start rather than jointly chosen with the form; and the byte objective is pre-zstd — notably, rows _are_ sorted by start_time yet starts still ship as flat u32 offsets, where a second-order encoding (deltas of sorted starts, guaranteed non-negative and mostly tiny) plus bit-packing would strictly dominate, with zstd recovering only part of the difference. Forms are discriminated by TILE_META keys, so the whole mechanism is frame-path only (the standalone layer shape takes none of them, encode.rs:124-131).

**Class.** Lightweight column-encoding selection in columnar stores (Parquet/C-Store choosing plain/delta/RLE per column — per-column polynomial; joint selection with dictionary/template budgets NP-hard in general); minimum description length (data cost + model cost); the template-coupling term makes it clustering-with-cardinality-penalty (facility-location flavored). Offline, deterministic, small discrete menus — exactly solvable by per-layer enumeration plus a global template accounting pass.

**Knobs.** `--no-compact-times` (kill switch; default OFF = feature ON, declares the `time-delta` capability); no precision knob — the encoding is lossless by construction.

**Gap.** Add narrower tiers (U16 offsets/durations, bucket-anchored offsets) gated on the same feasibility pattern plus explicit template-count accounting — for archives with seconds-to-minutes buckets the u32 start column is 2× oversized. Measurable objective: core-column bytes per pack at zero fidelity cost, under the constraint that added shapes don't blow up `manifest.schemas` (the formulation's $c_{tmpl}$ term made explicit rather than special-cased). Not implemented, not planned.

## 3. Tile payload encoding & quantization

This subsystem decides how the bytes inside a single tile are spent: which numeric representation each geometry and property column ships in (crates/stt-core/src/arrow_tile/{quantize,encode,columns,config,frame}.rs, crates/stt-build/src/{columnar,props}.rs), what order the rows are written in, which precomputed sidecars ride along, and where metadata lives. Every choice trades compressed archive bytes against decode exactness, client CPU, schema-template count, and — the recurring non-obvious coupling — content-addressed pack dedup, which only fires when equal content encodes to equal bytes across tiles. Temporal column compaction (vertex-time delta tiers, start/end-time form selection) was moved out of this section (see §2 — The temporal axis).

### 3.1 Coordinate quantization on a world-anchored grid

**Setting.** With `--quantize-coords <m>` set, every geometry vertex is snapped to a single global lattice anchored at (−180, −90) with uniform step $m/111320$ degrees, and the Float64 GeoArrow leaf is replaced by an i32 grid-index leaf; the reconstruction affine ships in field metadata (`stt:quant`, placed at encode.rs:333-379) and both reference readers apply $\mathrm{lon} = x_0 + q_x s_x$. The grid is deliberately dataset- and tile-independent — not per-tile-bbox-relative — so identical geometry encodes byte-identically in every tile; per-tile grids were measured at +61% archive size on a dedup-heavy dataset because they break content-addressed pack dedup (quantize.rs:137-142). A precision floor (~18.7 mm) rejects steps whose ±180° longitude index would overflow i32 (quantize.rs:109-132). The 3D variant (`EncoderConfig::point_elevation_column`, config.rs:122-140) adds a z axis at the same metre step with a fixed $z_0 = 0$ datum, again for cross-tile dedup; an out-of-range z index is a hard error, never a silent clamp (quantize.rs:95-107). Default OFF is byte-identical to the historical Float64 encoder.

**Problem.** Given a dataset of features with WGS84 vertices $(x_v, y_v[, z_v])$, tiled into tiles $t$ with vertex multisets $V_t$; a max zoom $z_{\max}$; a content-addressed pack store (equal blob bytes are stored once); and the meters-per-degree constant $M = 111320$. Choose a quantizer $Q = (o_x, o_y, s_x, s_y[, o_z, s_z], w)$ with origins $o \in \mathbb{R}$, steps $s > 0$ (degrees per quantum; metres for $z$), leaf width $w \in \{16, 32, 64\}$ bits, applied as $q = \mathrm{round}((x - o)/s)$; or $Q = \bot$ (raw Float64). In principle $Q$ may vary per tile ($Q_t$). Minimize total stored archive bytes

$$B(Q) = \sum_{b \in \mathcal{B}(Q)} |\mathrm{zstd}(b)|$$

where $\mathcal{B}(Q)$ is the set of _distinct_ tile blobs after content-addressed dedup — so $B$ depends on whether identical geometry encodes to identical bytes across tiles. Subject to:

1. Distortion: worst-case ground error $\le \varepsilon$, i.e. $\tfrac{s_y}{2} M \le \varepsilon$ (latitude) and $\tfrac{s_x}{2} M \cos\varphi \le \varepsilon$ at every latitude $\varphi$ in the data; the natural target is $\varepsilon \le$ half a screen pixel's ground size at $z_{\max}$.
2. Index range: $\max_v |q_v| \le 2^{w-1} - 1$; for a global grid at $w = 32$ this forces the step floor $m \ge 360 M / (2^{31} - 1) \approx 18.7\,\mathrm{mm}$.
3. Dedup preservation: $Q_t = Q_{t'}$ for all tiles $t, t'$ (a translation-invariant global lattice), so equal vertex runs produce equal bytes.
4. Exact decode: the reader reconstructs via the published affine $\hat{x} = o + qs$; error is bounded, never structural.

**Incumbent.** In the builder, a single user-chosen precision $m$ with everything else fixed by constant: `world_grid_affine` builds the global lattice (quantize.rs:146-159), 3D twin at quantize.rs:166-173, floor guard `validate_quantize_coords_m` (quantize.rs:109-132), rounding + i32 clamp in `qx`/`qy` (quantize.rs:82-88), leaf swap Float64→Int32 in `build_geometry_array_q` (columns.rs:63-187). Leaf width is hard-coded to 32 bits even when a coarser dataset would fit 16 — deliberate, since the tile-anchored 16-bit alternative costs the +61% dedup loss above. The choice of $m$ itself has an incumbent in stt-optimize's quantize advisor: it derives _the_ candidate as a quarter-pixel of ground resolution at the recommended max zoom, $q/2 \le g(z,\varphi)/4$ with $g(z,\varphi) = \tfrac{2\pi R \cos\varphi}{256 \cdot 2^{z}}$, snaps it down a 6-rung ladder $\{5, 1, 0.5, 0.1, 0.05, 0.01\}$ m, trial-encodes the ≤5000-feature sample through the production encoder, and advises only on a ≥5% measured shrink (≥15% earns high confidence); being lossy it is never auto-applied (advisors/quantize.rs:26-240, one-point evaluation at quantize.rs:67-112). Greedy/hardcoded: one Lagrangian point on the rate–distortion curve, never a ladder sweep; indices get no entropy coding beyond downstream per-blob zstd.

**Class.** Rate–distortion theory; uniform scalar quantization (within ~0.25 bit/sample of entropy-coded at high rate for bounded support); lattice quantization. The dedup-preservation constraint is a deterministic-encoding side constraint absent from classical R–D — the true objective is only evaluable by re-encoding, though the 1-D search over $m$ at fixed structure is cheap. Offline, deterministic.

**Knobs.** `--quantize-coords <meters>` (default off = Float64); floor `MIN_QUANTIZE_COORDS_M` ≈ 0.0187 m; `M_PER_DEG_LAT = 111320` (quantize.rs:14); `point_elevation_column` (config.rs:122-140); legacy one-shot atomic `QUANTIZE_COORDS_UM` (config.rs:23-47). Advisor side: ladder $\{5,\dots,0.01\}$ m, quarter-pixel divisor 4, `MIN_COORD_SHRINK` 0.05, `HIGH_CONFIDENCE_SHRINK` 0.15.

**Gap.** (a) The advisor evaluates a single R–D point; sweeping the 6-rung ladder (six extra sample encodes) would expose the empirical knee, which may sit far from quarter-pixel — coarse-zoom fleets (z0-6) could take much coarser grids at zero visible loss. Measurable objective: compressed pack bytes at fixed max-zoom pixel error. (b) 16-bit tile-anchored deltas are ruled out only because dedup is blob-granular; the roadmap's geometry-blob sharing (PLANNED) would restore dedup at sub-blob granularity and re-open the 2× leaf saving. (c) No study of step choice vs zstd interaction — coarser grids yield more repeated indices and better compression; the true objective already rewards this but nothing searches it.

### 3.2 Property type inference and schema unification

**Setting.** Each user property key must be assigned exactly one wire kind — Numeric (Float64/quantized) or Categorical (Utf8/Dictionary) — holding in _every_ tile, including all-null tiles. Per layer, `PropertyAccumulator` runs two passes: `observe` accumulates per-key flags {has_number, has_numeric_string, has_other} (numeric-looking strings like `"1000.0"` count as numeric — a known producer bug deliberately absorbed), then `seal` freezes the split with the rule numeric ⟺ (number ∨ numeric-string) ∧ ¬other (columnar.rs:529-780, rule at 605 and 733). Because per-tile sniffing drifts (an all-null-in-tile column vanishes; a numeric-strings-only tile flips kind), a dataset-wide pre-pass `infer_property_types` runs the same rule over all features and pins the kinds via `ColumnarOptions::property_types` (columnar.rs:594-616, 79-96); declared source-schema kinds are authoritative and never overridden, with `fill_property_type_gaps` filling only untyped keys (columnar.rs:632-648). Upstream, `PropertyTableBuilder` holds ingested values in typed columnar batches — type fixed by first non-null value, lossless demote-to-JSON escape hatch, serde int/float distinction preserved exactly (props.rs:121-135, 230-292, 344-363); this ingest refactor cut 4M-row builds from 11.63 to 6.71 GB by eliminating a ~830 B/row BTreeMap leaf.

**Problem.** Given keys $K$; for each key $\kappa$ a dataset value multiset $V_\kappa$ with per-value type observations in $\{\mathrm{num}, \mathrm{numstr}, \mathrm{other}\}$; a partition of the dataset into tiles; a declared partial map $\delta: K' \rightharpoonup \{\mathrm{Num}, \mathrm{Cat}\}$ from the source schema; and a validator that fails cross-tile column type/presence variation. Choose a total labeling $L: K \to \{\mathrm{Num}, \mathrm{Cat}\}$ and a per-value coercion map (e.g. `"1000.0"` → 1000.0; bool → `"true"`). Objective, lexicographic: (1) zero corruption — every value of $\kappa$ representable under $L(\kappa)$ without loss of downstream meaning; (2) maximize analytic utility $|\{\kappa : L(\kappa) = \mathrm{Num}\}|$ weighted by use (numeric columns drive color ramps/elevation; categorical cannot); (3) minimize distinct schema templates (tile-invariance makes this exactly one per layer shape). Subject to: $L(\kappa) = \delta(\kappa)$ wherever declared; $L$ tile-invariant by construction (a single dataset-level function); an all-null-in-tile column still emitted with kind $L(\kappa)$; any $\mathrm{other}$-typed value forces $L(\kappa) = \mathrm{Cat}$.

**Incumbent.** The observe/seal rule is a monotone join on a 2-point lattice — a least-fixed-point computation, linear time, and optimal _for the stated rule_; coercion in `value_as_f64` (columnar.rs:565-571). What is heuristic is the rule itself: all-or-nothing coercion per column, a binary kind set, and no cost weighing between the lexicographic tiers.

**Class.** Type inference / schema unification as lattice join (linear-time, unique LFP); minimum-description-length model selection for the rule-design question; schema matching. Deterministic, offline (the streaming builder already holds all features resident).

**Knobs.** `AttributeFilter` include/exclude/exclude-all gates which keys exist at all (columnar.rs:22-60); no thresholds — the classification rule is hard-coded.

**Gap.** The binary label set loses information: booleans become dictionary strings instead of a 1-bit numeric 0/1 (the code tells producers to emit 0/1 themselves, columnar.rs:696-699); a 99.9%-numeric column with one stray word goes wholly Categorical, losing ramps/elevation for the entire dataset. A robust-typing formulation — Numeric + per-row null-out or exception sidecar, chosen when corruption count ≤ θ and utility gain dominates — would strictly improve utility at bounded loss (NOT PLANNED; gap analysis). Measurable objective: count of columns usable for numeric styling + validator drift findings, at zero corrupted values.

### 3.3 Per-column numeric-property encoding selection

**Setting.** Each numeric column ships raw Float64 or as fixed-point integers under a per-column affine $v = o + qs$ recorded in `stt:qa`/TILE_META.qa. Two triggers: an explicit precision map (`--quantize-attr name=prec`) and an auto mode (`--quantize-attrs-auto`, default off). The auto path is a per-tile rule tree: refuse (stay Float64) iff any $|v| \ge$ i32::MAX — the _only_ refusal, chosen because magnitude is a property of the column's domain, not the tile's row sample, which keeps the quantize-or-not verdict tile-stable (stt-validate hard-fails a Float64↔integer Arrow-type flip as structural drift); else exact frame-of-reference at step 1 with the narrowest leaf when all values are integers and fit; else range-adaptive UInt16 with $s = \mathrm{span}/65535$ (quantize.rs:462-516). The explicit path snaps a requested step ≥ 1 down to exact step-1 only when that keeps the leaf at UInt16 (quantize.rs:547-615, gate 559-570), and hard-errors on overflow rather than clamping (577-585). A sibling opt-in lever (`quantize_vertex_values`) re-types per-vertex List\<Float32\> columns — the only columns with no other size lever, measured at 64–94% of some tiles — to List\<UInt16\> under a per-tile range-adaptive affine with a reserved 0xFFFF NaN sentinel (quantize.rs:220-262, columns.rs:516-586, decode re-inflation decode.rs:311-325).

**Problem.** Given, for each numeric property $j$, tile samples $S_{j,t} \subset \mathbb{R} \cup \{\mathrm{null}\}$ with $n_t$ rows and dataset domain $D_j = \bigcup_t S_{j,t}$; a validator that treats a Float64↔integer type flip across tiles as hard failure but tolerates UInt16↔Int32 width flips; and an error tolerance $\varepsilon_j$ (∞ for display-only measurements, $0$ for identifier domains). Choose per column and tile an encoding $e_{j,t} \in E = \{\mathrm{F64}\} \cup \{(o, s, \ell) : o \in \mathbb{R},\, s > 0,\, \ell \in \{\mathrm{U16}, \mathrm{I32}\}\}$ with $q_i = \mathrm{round}((v_i - o)/s)$, decode $\hat{v}_i = o + q_i s$. Minimize

$$\sum_j \sum_t \Big( n_t \cdot \tfrac{w(e_{j,t})}{8} + \mathrm{meta}(e_{j,t}) \Big), \qquad w(\mathrm{F64}) = 64,\ w(\mathrm{U16}) = 16,\ w(\mathrm{I32}) = 32$$

(ideally the post-zstd bytes rather than raw widths). Subject to:

1. Error: $|\hat{v}_i - v_i| \le s/2 \le \varepsilon_j$ for all values; exactness required when $\varepsilon_j = 0$.
2. Schema stability (measurable selection): $\mathbb{1}[e_{j,t} = \mathrm{F64}]$ must be a function of $D_j$ alone, not of $S_{j,t}$ — constant over $t$. Width $\ell$ may vary.
3. Leaf range: $\max_i q_i \le 65535$ (U16) or $2^{31}-1$ (I32), with $o$ chosen so $q_i \ge 0$.
4. Determinism: identical columns quantize identically across tiles (content-addressed dedup).

**Incumbent.** Rule tree `build_quantized_numeric_auto` (quantize.rs:462-516) with the sole refusal at `AUTO_QUANT_MAX_ABS = i32::MAX` (quantize.rs:275-314 — motivated by a measured corruption: nyc-taxi-points `trip_id` shipped at $s = 2.3 \times 10^{14}$, every id wrong by up to $\pm 1.15 \times 10^{14}$); encode-side dispatch encode.rs:518-549. Greedy/per-tile: the affine offset is the _per-tile_ minimum, so the same value decodes differently in different tiles under lossy mode; the step menu is only $\{1, \mathrm{span}/65535\}$; no dataset-wide statistics are used.

**Class.** Frame-of-reference encoding and per-column encoding selection from column stores; per-column scalar quantizer design (rate–distortion: optimal bits $b_j = \lceil \log_2(\mathrm{span}_j / 2\varepsilon_j + 1) \rceil$); the tile-invariance requirement is a measurable-selection constraint turning a per-sample optimization into one over the column's domain. Per-tile O(n); the principled version is two-pass offline.

**Knobs.** `--quantize-attr name=prec` (config.rs:48-66); `--quantize-attrs-auto`, default OFF (config.rs:76-88); `quantize_vertex_values`, default OFF, frame-path only (config.rs:191-213); leaf menu fixed {U16, I32}; sentinel 0xFFFF / max finite index 65534.

**Gap.** PLANNED (explicitly deferred in docs/roadmap/stt-packed-format-decisions.md, cited at quantize.rs:306-313): a dataset-wide range pre-pass making the affine global per column — fixing (i) cross-tile decode inconsistency of the same value, (ii) the documented outlier failure (a body of 0–10 with one rare $10^6$ collapses onto one index; detecting it per tile would reintroduce drift, so it is currently an advertised cost), and (iii) enabling true per-column rate selection (arbitrary $b_j$, e.g. 8- or 24-bit packed leaves) beyond the two-leaf menu. Measurable objective: compressed property-column bytes at fixed $\varepsilon_j$, plus elimination of cross-tile inconsistency. Also unmodeled: post-zstd size — a step-1 FOR column of small ints compresses far better than raw Float64, so the true gain exceeds the 4× width ratio, but nothing measures per-column entropy.

### 3.4 Categorical column encoding — dictionary vs plain Utf8

**Setting.** For every string column, per tile, the encoder builds the candidate dictionary (categories deduplicated in first-seen order for run-to-run stability, UInt16 keys) and chooses Dictionary\<UInt16,Utf8\> vs plain Utf8 by comparing modeled _pre-compression_ IPC byte costs — deliberately conservative, to avoid encoding the tile twice (encode.rs:53-71). More than 65535 distinct values overflows the U16 key space: `build_dictionary_indices` errors descriptively (the previous behavior silently collapsed overflow values onto one index), and the encode path degrades that error to plain Utf8 rather than failing the tile (columns.rs:270-311; `.ok()` at encode.rs:551-584).

**Problem.** Given a tile's column values $S = (s_1, \dots, s_n)$ (strings, with nulls), distinct categories $C = \{c_1, \dots, c_k\}$; the downstream compressor zstd; and the template registry where each distinct Arrow schema (Utf8 vs Dictionary _is_ a schema difference) costs one stored template. Choose $e_t \in \{\mathrm{Utf8}, \mathrm{Dict}\langle \mathrm{U16}, \mathrm{Utf8}\rangle\}$ per column per tile. Minimize the true archive contribution

$$|\mathrm{zstd}(\mathrm{tile}(e_t))| + \Delta_{\mathrm{tmpl}}(e_{\cdot})$$

where $\Delta_{\mathrm{tmpl}}$ is the amortized cost of every additional distinct PROPS schema template the per-tile choices fork across the dataset. Subject to: Dict feasible iff $k \le 65535$; exact strings preserved either way; encoding deterministic given the values (first-seen category order) so identical tiles dedup.

**Incumbent.** `categorical_dictionary_is_smaller` compares the surrogate $\mathrm{plain} = \sum_i |s_i| + 4(n{+}1)$ vs $\mathrm{dict} = \sum_j |c_j| + 4(k{+}1) + 2n + 192$, with `CATEGORICAL_DICTIONARY_IPC_OVERHEAD = 192` a fixed, unmeasured framing allowance (encode.rs:47-71). Approximations: (1) the model is pre-compression — plain Utf8 with heavy repeats compresses toward the dictionary size under zstd anyway, so the model overstates the dictionary's post-compression advantage; (2) the decision is per-tile and value-dependent, so one column can be Dict in dense tiles and Utf8 in sparse ones — forking PROPS templates whose cost no term in the comparison sees.

**Class.** Dictionary/codebook selection; per-column encoding selection in column stores (C-Store-style), where interactions with the downstream compressor and schema versioning make the exact problem non-separable; here per-column O(n) with an O(1) model. Deterministic, offline.

**Knobs.** Overhead constant 192 B; key width fixed U16 (cap 65535 categories); no dataset-level override.

**Gap.** A post-zstd-aware model (or one trial compression on a sample) would flip boundary decisions correctly; a dataset-global verdict per column (compute $k$ and length stats over all tiles, pick once) would eliminate template forking and cross-tile type flips at negligible size cost. The bigger prize — a dataset-shared dictionary with keys-only tiles — is the DEFERRED dictionary hoist treated in §3.8. Measurable objective: compressed PROPS bytes + manifest.schemas entry count.

### 3.5 Intra-tile row permutation under the sorted-times contract

**Setting.** Before writing the Arrow batch, the encoder stable-sorts each layer's rows by `start_time` (`sort_rows_by_start_time`, encode.rs:791-867, invoked from `encode_layer_v2_parts` at encode.rs:889 — after the tiler assigned feature ids, so ids stay order-independent; a fast path returns the layer borrowed when rows are already non-decreasing, encode.rs:797-798). Every column rides the same permutation — geometry, properties, vertex-nested lists, triangles; feature-local index sidecars (`triangles`, `polygon_parts`) move verbatim with no rebasing (encode.rs:857-864). The sort buys two things: the tile declares `TILE_META.sorted` (frame.rs:230-234), surfaced to the reader as `timesSorted` (packages/core/src/tile.ts:869-870, 1921), enabling monotone time-window slicing instead of full scans; and the delta-coded start-time forms chosen at encode shrink, since sorted deltas are non-negative and small (see §2 — temporal column compaction).

**Problem.** Given a tile layer with $n$ rows; columns $c \in C$, each with a byte-serialization function $B_c(\pi)$ of the row permutation $\pi \in S_n$; the reader contract $S(\pi) = [\text{start\_time non-decreasing under } \pi]$; and a reader-side query cost $q$ for time-window extraction without the sorted declaration (an O(n) scan per window). Choose $\pi \in S_n$ and a declaration bit $d \in \{0, 1\}$ to minimize

$$\sum_{c \in C} |\mathrm{zstd}(B_c(\pi))| \;+\; \lambda\,(1 - d)\, q$$

subject to: $d = 1 \Rightarrow S(\pi)$ (the declaration must be truthful); all columns permute jointly (one $\pi$ for the whole layer — Arrow rows are atomic); feature-local sidecar indices are permutation-invariant in content, so correctness imposes no further constraint. The residual freedom under $d = 1$ is exactly the tie order within each equal-`start_time` group: ties do not change the time column's delta sequence, but they do reorder geometry and property bytes, so the constrained problem decomposes into independent ordering problems within each group, trading nothing on the time column against spatial coherence of geometry bytes under zstd. The unconstrained relaxation ($d = 0$) opens the full $S_n$ — e.g. a space-filling-curve order maximizing geometry coherence — at the price of time-column delta entropy and the per-query scan cost $q$.

**Incumbent.** $\pi$ = stable sort by `start_time` with ties fixed by upstream tiler placement order (encode.rs:800-801); $d = 1$ always (`sorted` is "always true from this encoder", frame.rs:230-234). No search over tie-breaks (e.g. a secondary Hilbert order of geometry within equal timestamps), and no joint consideration of geometry-column coherence — the permutation is chosen entirely for the time column and the reader contract.

**Class.** Row reordering for columnar compression is a minimum-linear-arrangement / TSP-flavored problem, NP-hard in general; the sorted-contract version reduces to optimal ordering within equal-key groups (still MinLA-flavored per group). Deterministic, offline, per tile.

**Knobs.** None — the sort is unconditional; the only lever on tie order is the upstream placement order the tiler happens to produce.

**Gap.** NOT PLANNED (gap analysis): a secondary spatial sort key within equal-`start_time` groups is _free_ with respect to the time column and the reader contract, and would improve geometry-byte coherence under zstd on datasets with coarse time buckets (many ties). Measurable objective: compressed tile bytes at an unchanged `sorted` contract. The sort also interacts with feature-id monotonicity — row-index ids are assigned before the permutation (§3.6).

### 3.6 Feature-id assignment for compressed size

**Setting.** Every feature ships a u64 `id` column (picking + cross-tile identity). `determine_feature_id` (columnar.rs:1122-1145): an explicit numeric source id is preserved verbatim; a string id is hashed with spec-fixed FNV-1a-64 (deliberately not DefaultHasher — a toolchain bump changing hashes would change every tile byte and content address, breaking incremental-deploy economics); a POINT feature with no source id gets the per-tile row index instead of the historical hash of (time, lon, lat) — points are never split across tiles so need no globally-stable id, and the high-entropy hash was measured as the single largest column in Waymo LiDAR tiles (~40% of compressed point bytes), while monotone row indices compress to a few bits per point (columnar.rs:272-283). Clipped line/trajectory segments keep hash-derived ids because one source feature's pieces must carry the _same_ id in every tile they span (`segment_feature_id`, columnar.rs:1148-1170).

**Problem.** Given features $f$ partitioned into tiles (a clipped feature appears in several); an equivalence relation $\sim$ (pieces of one source feature); the compressor zstd; and the consumer contracts of picking (per-tile uniqueness) and cross-tile identity for $\sim$-classes. Choose a labeling $\iota: f \mapsto \mathbb{Z}_{2^{64}}$ to minimize

$$\sum_t |\mathrm{zstd}(\mathrm{idcol}_t(\iota))|$$

— the compressed id-column bytes across all tiles, well-approximated by the empirical entropy of the per-tile delta sequence in row order. Subject to:

1. Uniqueness: $\iota$ injective within each (tile, layer).
2. Identity: $f \sim g \Rightarrow \iota(f) = \iota(g)$.
3. Determinism: $\iota$ a pure, toolchain-stable function of the source (rebuild reproducibility, content-address stability).
4. Source fidelity: consumer-meaningful source ids stay recoverable.

**Incumbent.** The hard-coded per-geometry-kind policy above.
